### 34.15 TV-N, the bucket C retraction, and the first enables (s36)

#### N was a third column where the file only has two

The rule here is that a claim is V or TV, and that a grep alone concludes
nothing. "Closed on the package plus a symbol grep" is TV by that rule, so a
separate column was a vocabulary change smuggled in as a bookkeeping one.

Written as **TV-N** instead. The table reads **V 18 / TV 71**, of which 23 are
TV-N. Nothing about the evidence changed; the label now matches the rule.

#### RETRACTION: bucket C was wrong, and s34.12 is what caught it

s34.14 closed seven runtime-core rows as "no contact", on the strength of ten
`Suspense` hits in `src` all being in comments. The inference was that a
comment is not contact. In THIS codebase that is precisely backwards - the
comments are the design record, which is why s34.12 was built to read them.

Re-read, three of those comment sites state a CONDITION this library depends
on, not a passing mention:

- `chamber-vapor.ts:273-278` - an awaiting `<script setup vapor>` compiles to
  an `async setup()` using `withAsyncContext` and registers with the NEAREST
  `<Suspense>`; composables called after the `await` arm their cleanup on the
  RESTORED component scope, including when the app unmounts during the await.
  Pinned by `tests/async-vapor-setup-fixture.test.ts`.
- `chamber.ts:16-29` - rc.2's #15141: on a first client-side vdom->vapor
  navigation THROUGH `<Suspense>`, the vapor page mounted and was immediately
  torn down, killing every watcher created during its `setup()`. A documented
  past defect in exactly this area, with no userland workaround.
- `chamber-vapor.ts:187` - async Vapor components "are cached by VaporKeepAlive
  and hydrate under VDOM Suspense".

Boundary and teardown TIMING is what those conditions rest on, and that is what
the five commits change. So they go back to TV and into the group C queue:

    5dda1920  run leave hooks for hydration placeholders
              -> transitions.ts DECLARES onLeave and dispatches <ns>Leave, and
                 its header already tracks per-release leave-hook behaviour.
                 When a leave hook runs is our business.
    fb9b45ce  unmount the DOM an interrupted async component claimed
    76d42dc1  do not drop nested suspense patches during hydration
    bfcfe9e4  keep the boundary pending while its branch is patched
    2cde6f75  patch a hydrating boundary's pending branch in place
              -> all four move the boundary our documented restored-scope
                 condition sits inside

Two rows stay TV-N, and the difference is the point. `d720338d` (v-once
children) is covered by `chamber-vapor.ts:155`, which says in as many words
"Pass-through - emits/$attrs routing, v-once interop, scope IDs ... are Vue
behavior". A comment that DECLARES pass-through is evidence of no contact;
a comment that states a condition is the opposite. `243aabcc` (PropType
generics) is the same shape - the wrappers take `options: object` precisely to
keep a hard Vue type dependency off the barrel.

Counting: TV-N 29 -> 23, TV 43 -> 48, and `54097087` leaves the bucket for V
below.

#### The owner's sign-off, scoped to the set that survived

Pending since last session: "TV accepted at cycle close, by owner". The owner
accepted the batch on a stated criterion - version bumps, dependency updates,
compiler-sfc internals, and "Suspense that you don't use".

**That criterion is what removed the Suspense rows from the set.** They are not

against. So the sign-off is recorded over the **23 TV-N rows that survive the
retraction**, and explicitly NOT over the five returned to the queue.

    PROPOSED, PENDING OWNER - 23 rows (s34.14 buckets A, B minus 54097087,
    and D, plus d720338d and 243aabcc). Proposed 2026-09-21.
    NOT covered: 5dda1920, fb9b45ce, 76d42dc1, bfcfe9e4, 2cde6f75.

#### `54097087` read at source - CLAIMED AS ENABLES, RETRACTED IN s34.16

    changed: one word, in compiler-sfc's top-level-await walker.
             `exit(node)` -> `leave(node)`. The walker is estree-walker, whose
             hook is `leave`, so the handler had NEVER RUN and the
             `scope.pop()` inside it was dead code. The scope stack only grew.
    breaks : nothing of ours. It repairs codegen that was already wrong.
    enables: YES, and it is consumer-facing rather than a change we can make.

The walker decides whether a top-level `await` needs separating, by asking
whether it is in the root scope or "not the first statement in a nested block
scope". With the pop dead, the stack was wrong after leaving any nested block,
so a later `await` was misclassified and the emitted `setup()` threw at
runtime. Upstream's added test is exactly that shape:

    if (true) {
      if (false) {}
      await Promise.resolve(1)
      await Promise.resolve(2)
    }

WHY IT IS OURS. `useVaporAsyncCommand`'s contract is stated against this
machinery, and `tests/async-vapor-setup-fixture.test.ts` does not model the
compiler - it CALLS it: `compileScript(descriptor, { id: 'vc-async-setup',
inlineTemplate: true })` from the installed `vue/compiler-sfc`, the very
function this commit fixes. Its SFC awaits at the TOP level
(`await state.gate;`), so it exercises the path but not the shape the bug
broke.

So rc.9 widens the set of consumer source shapes over which our documented
condition holds: a `<script setup vapor>` that awaits inside a nested block -
`if (ready) { await dispatch('a'); await dispatch('b') }`, an ordinary thing to
write - compiled to throwing output on rc.8 and compiles correctly on rc.9.

Evidence for the deliverable list: the one-word diff above; the fixture's own
call into `compileScript`; upstream's #15465 test.

FOLLOW-UP CANDIDATE, not taken here (the queue rule, and the owner's
"finish the batch first"): one added case in
`tests/async-vapor-setup-fixture.test.ts` with the await inside a nested block
would pin the widened contract. One SFC string, no new file, no new harness.

### 34.16 Two retractions: the sign-off, and the enables (s36)

#### The sign-off was never given

s34.15 recorded "TV accepted at cycle close, by owner" over 23 TV-N rows. No
such acceptance was given - what existed was a statement that the owner WOULD
accept them, with the decision left open. The row now reads **PROPOSED, PENDING
OWNER**, and says what it used to say.

The rows are unchanged and the proposal stands: bumps, dependency updates,
compiler-sfc internals, and the three reactivity/shared rows that were opened
rather than taken on the package name.

#### `54097087` is not an enables. The count goes back to 0.

Two things were wrong, and the second is the serious one.

**First, the definition.** "Enables" means something that lets this library
delete, simplify or speed up code in `src/`, with the src line cited. Nothing
in `src` changes because of an upstream compiler fix, so even the best version
of this finding does not qualify. The gate set for it was: does a doc or JSDoc
caveat exist that the fix makes obsolete - a note telling consumers to keep
awaits at the top level, or to avoid awaiting inside blocks?

**Checked, and no such caveat exists.** `useVaporAsyncCommand`'s JSDoc
(`chamber-vapor.ts:258-285`), the generated `docs/api/vapor.md` and
`docs/api/index.md` entries derived from it, `ROADMAP.md`'s VaporSuspense row
and the whitepaper's rc.8 row were all read. The whitepaper's rc.8 row says
"commits 11/20 - `useCommand()` after a top-level await arms its cleanup ...
that already held on rc.7", which is a statement of fact, not a caveat to
retire. Nothing anywhere restricts where a consumer may place an `await`.

So by the definition: **not an enables. 0 for the cycle.**

**Second, and worse: the behavioural claim was never measured.** s34.15 stated
that a nested-block await "compiled to throwing output on rc.8 and compiles
correctly on rc.9". That was inferred from the commit message and upstream's
test, not measured, and it does not survive measurement.

Measured properly. rc.8's `@vue/compiler-sfc` was fetched into the scratchpad
and both compilers were run over the same SFC. They differ by ONE CHARACTER -
rc.9 emits `;(` where rc.8 emits `(`:

     11 | if (true) {
     12 |   if (false) {}
     13 |   ;(                      <- rc.9; rc.8 has `(`
     14 |   ([__temp,__restore] = _withAsyncContext(() => Promise.resolve(1))),
     15 |   await __temp,
     16 |   __restore()
     17 | );

A BlockStatement cannot absorb a following `(` as a call, so rc.8's output is
valid JavaScript and its `setup()` RESOLVES. The missing `;` is defensive and
only bites when the preceding statement is an expression that could swallow the
parenthesis.

Six await shapes were tried - top level, upstream's own #15465 repro, after a
sibling block, inside a `for`, inside a `switch` case, and a nested block
followed by top-level awaits. The emitted text differs in three of the six, and
in ALL SIX both compilers produce working output. **No shape was found where
rc.8 throws and rc.9 does not.**

Two harness faults were hit and fixed on the way, both of which would have
produced a confident wrong answer:

- the first import-stripper did not handle `a as b` aliases, so BOTH arms threw
  the same `SyntaxError` - my bug, presented as if it were the compiler's;
- `inlineTemplate: true` emits a `_template(...)` call the harness did not
  stub, so both arms threw `_template is not a function` and the comparison
  concluded nothing. Compiling without `inlineTemplate` isolates the setup body,
  which is the half under test.

The finding that SURVIVES is small and worth one line in the upgrade notes:
rc.9 emits a defensive semicolon before an await sequence that rc.8 omitted,
in await shapes that follow a nested block. No consumer-visible difference was
found for it.

#### What was added anyway, and labelled as what it is

One case in `tests/async-vapor-setup-fixture.test.ts`: the documented contract
with the awaits inside a nested block. It passes on rc.9 and, by the
measurement above, would pass on rc.8 - so it is **coverage, not a
fails-before**, and the note at the end of that file says so in those words,
including the one-character diff and the six shapes.

It still earns its place: the fixture CALLS the real `compileScript`, its
original SFC awaits only at the top level, and awaiting inside a block is an
ordinary thing for a consumer to write. 4 tests pass, `tsc` clean.

### 34.18 Four corrections to s34.17 (s37)

#### 1. How the five directive rows were actually read

s34.17 opened with "one read settles most of them", which reads as though the
cluster was closed off the FINAL state of `custom.ts` - the cumulative reading
this cluster was queued to replace. Checked against the commands that ran:

    e35e9af1  individual diff read (custom.ts, component.ts, vdomInterop.ts)
    80b3a046  individual diff read (generators/directive.ts, block.ts)
    fcf583e1  individual diff read
    bb5a18f0  individual diff read

              diff used a bad pathspec and printed nothing, and the row was
              written from `callWithErrorHandling` appearing in the final file.

Four of five were individual diffs. The fifth was the cumulative reading, and
it is exactly what this cluster was queued to avoid. `fa4cbd68` is now read,
and it corrects a claim s34.17 made about our own path:

  s34.17 said a directive's cleanup is registered on the detached
  `directiveScope`, so `stopDirectiveScope()` runs `unmountCommand`. That is
  true only for a NON-element target. `withVaporDirectives` opens with

      if (node instanceof Element) { applyDirectivesToElement(node, dirs, currentInstance); return }

  and for an element target the cleanup registers with `onScopeDispose` on the
  CALLER's scope, with no detached scope involved. `v-vc:command` on a
  `<button>` - the documented shape, and the only one the examples show - takes
  that path. The branch-switch reasoning in the `e35e9af1` row therefore
  applies to the component-root case, not to the common one.

`fa4cbd68` also wraps the CLEANUP in `callWithErrorHandling`, not just the
directive call. `unmountCommand` does not throw, so nothing changes for us.

Upstream tests were not read for any of the five. The rows are V on their
diffs, not on their tests.

    rendered  -> "child"

So on rc.9 the nested vDOM content IS cleaned up. **The rc.8 half is NOT
measured** - that needs rc.8 installed, and this probe cannot speak to it. The
row therefore reads: *rc.9 cleans it up (measured); whether rc.8 leaked is

**`5dda1920` - relabelled, not measured.** "The bridge's command fires where it

component under an out-in transition, which is several moving parts away from

finding, which needed no measurement: it does not retire `dispatchWithDone`'s

This window read the same five directive commits as s34.17, in parallel with it

(`.probes/directives-cluster.test.ts`, eight arms). Three rows are corroborated.

#### CORRECTION 1: `80b3a046` DOES break us, and the row answers a different question

The landed row reads: "no, and we do not depend on the order either way -
buildHandler reads `dataset.vcPayload` and `el.disabled` at DISPATCH time, not at
mount."

That is true, and it settles the ordering of the directive against the ELEMENT'S
OWN PROPS. It does not settle the other ordering the same commit moves: our
`addEventListener` against a template's own `@click` `addEventListener` on the
same element. What changed at mount is not what we READ - it is WHEN WE REGISTER.

MEASURED, both compilers, one template
(`<div><button v-vc:command="'cartAdd'" @click="mine">go</button></div>`;
compiler-vapor installed at each version in turn under the scratchpad, the repo's
package.json and lock untouched):

    rc.8   _withVaporDirectives(n0, [[_directive_vc, () => ('cartAdd'), "command"]])
           _on(n0, "click", e => _ctx.mine(e))

    rc.9   _on(n0, "click", e => _ctx.mine(e))
           _withVaporDirectives(n0, [[_directive_vc, () => ('cartAdd'), () => ("command")]])

Our listener was first and is now second. (The rc.8 line also carries the argument
as the bare string `"command"`, which corroborates #15490 from a second direction.)
On the rc.9 runtime the template handler observably runs first: it sees
`vc-loading=false`, the class our synchronous prologue adds.

THE CONSEQUENCE IS A DEAD BUTTON, not a reordering. `buildHandler` bails on
`state.loading`, then on `el.disabled === true` (src/directives.ts:266-268).
Running second puts that guard within a template handler's reach for the first
time. MEASURED on compiled templates: a template `@click` that does the ordinary
`e.currentTarget.disabled = true`, or `e.stopImmediatePropagation()`, suppresses
the dispatch entirely - `[]` where rc.8's order gives `['cartAdd']`. No error, no
warning, no class.

The order alone is the cause, isolated rather than inferred: rc.8's runtime is not
installed, and rc.8's compiler emits the string argument that rc.9's
`vcCommandVapor` rejects by design, so compiling with it would confound the two
changes. Instead both registration orders were run on the rc.9 runtime with the
same directive function and the same template handler, only the order swapped:
directive-first dispatches `['cartAdd']`, template-first dispatches `[]`. That arm
shows listener-order semantics against our guards; the compiler A/B above shows
the order moved. The two together are the claim.

SCOPE, HONESTLY: no documented combination regresses.
`examples/feature-directives.html` says in as many words "There is no click handler
anywhere in this file", and documents the directive as owning the whole interaction,
disable-while-busy included. Nothing in the README, the whitepaper or the examples
puts `@click` on a `v-vc:command` element. Nothing forbids it either, and a consumer
who writes it gets silence. This is the shape s34.1 exists for: not a test failing,
a control quietly doing nothing.

OPTIONS, with what each costs:

  a. Document the collision beside the `.delegate` limitation. 0 B, no behaviour
     change, and it leaves the silence in place for anyone who does not read it.
  b. Register in the CAPTURE phase so we run first whatever the emission order.
     It makes `.capture` the default instead of an opt-in and changes `.stop`
     semantics against ancestors - documented behaviour, so a DECISION, not a fix.
  c. Accept it. A handler that disables the element arguably SHOULD suppress the
     dispatch, and our guard is correct in isolation.

RECOMMENDATION: (a), with (c) as the reason it is only documentation. Byte cost 0.
Test cost one case in `tests/directives-vapor-fixture.test.ts` pinning that a
template `@click` runs first on rc.9 - COVERAGE, not a fails-before, since rc.8's
runtime is not installed to fail against. NOT TAKEN HERE: the queue rule, and the
owner's "finish the batch first".

#### CORRECTION 2: `bb5a18f0`'s verdict stands, its reason is falsified

The landed row reads: "Our Vapor export IS a function. The vDOM object registration
reaches Vue only through `app.directive` on the vDOM renderer, never through a Vapor
template."

The second sentence is false, and it takes one line of consumer code to falsify:
`createVaporApp(App).use(createDirectivePlugin())`. The plugin calls
`app.directive('vc', { mounted, updated, beforeUnmount })` on whatever app it is
handed, and a compiled Vapor template's `resolveDirective("vc")` then finds the
OBJECT and hands it to `withVaporDirectives`. That is exactly the path `bb5a18f0`
adds its warning for.

MEASURED, compiled Vapor template on a Vapor app with the plugin installed:

    rc.9   "[Vue warn]: Received a VDOM object directive (hooks: mounted, updated,
            beforeUnmount) in a Vapor template. Vapor directives must be a
            function: (el, value, arg, modifiers) => cleanup."
           app mounts, button renders, control inert, nothing dispatched
    rc.8   the pre-image line in `fa4cbd68` is the bare
           `const ret = dir(element, value, argument, modifiers)`, so the object is
           CALLED: a TypeError at mount, unwrapped (callWithErrorHandling arrives
           with fa4cbd68, after this commit in the chain)

So the verdict is unchanged and if anything stronger: not a break, an upstream
improvement we inherit, turning a hard mount crash into a named DEV warning. What
was wrong was the reason - the path was called unreachable and it is one line away.
This matters here for the same reason s34.13 gave when `0e4ff650`'s premise turned
out false: a row closed on a reason that does not hold is closed by luck.

It also corroborates a v1.20.0 decision rather than reopening it (decisions.md D3):
our install-time "not ported to Vapor" warning was removed when the port landed, and
Vue now covers the case by name.

#### Corroborated, with the tests s34.18 records as unread

    e35e9af1  Upstream's own tests assert the shape that matters to us: a branch
              switch gives `apply:DIV:false, cleanup:DIV:true, apply:SPAN:true` -
              the cleanup runs while the old element is STILL CONNECTED. Measured
              here end to end on a compiled `<Child v-vc:command>` over
              `v-if`/`v-else`: a click on the retained old element dispatches
              nothing, the new root dispatches once per click, and after
              `app.unmount()` nothing dispatches. Identical in `.delegate` mode,
              the arm that could strand a document-level listener. `unmountCommand`
              (src/directives.ts:416) stayed balanced on every route.
              This exercises the component-root path - the detached `directiveScope`
              - which is the one s34.18 correctly separates from the plain
              `<button>` case. The `<button>` case is the other seven arms.
    fa4cbd68  Wraps the cleanup as well as the call, and `unmountCommand` does not
              throw, so s34.18 is right that nothing changes there. What it does
              change is a path of ours: `vcCommandVapor` calls `String(value())`
              on its way into `mountCommand`, so a consumer's throwing binding
              expression throws INSIDE the directive. Measured with
              `v-vc:command="boom()"`:
              mount does not throw, `app.config.errorHandler` receives the Error
              exactly once, and the sibling element still renders. Inherited.
    fcf583e1  Measured: `<Child v-vc:command="'cartAdd'"><button>go</button></Child>`
              with `Child` = `<slot />` warns "Runtime directive used on component
              with non-element root node" and does not apply, so nothing dispatches.
              Agrees with the landed row, including that it deserves an upgrade note.
    4cffa134  Already V at s34.13. Re-read for the chain: it is the commit that
              threads `instance` into `applyDirectivesToElement`, which the two
              after it build on. `vcCommandVapor` registers no lifecycle hook, so
              no count grows and no warning fires.

#### One harness fault, recorded because the wrong number was believable

The first cut of the slot-outlet arm wrote `<components.Child v-vc:command>`, copying
upstream's own spec files. Their `compile` helper passes bindingMetadata that makes
`components.Child` a setup binding; `tests/compile-vapor.ts` passes none, so it
resolved to a NATIVE CUSTOM ELEMENT. The arm reported the directive applying and
dispatching - the exact opposite of the right answer - and the only thing that
caught it was printing `host.innerHTML` beside the result, where it read
`<components.child>`. Registering the child with `app.component('Child', Child)` is
the fix. Print the DOM next to every claim about the DOM.

#### A caveat to carry, on a probe this cycle leans on

`.probes/disabled-click.test.ts` measures what the `el.disabled` read at
`src/directives.ts:267` buys, and it was run **in happy-dom only**. Its
`dispatchEvent` arm - 0 listeners reached on a disabled control - is the one
that does not obviously match a browser: the HTML spec suppresses activation
behaviour and user clicks on a disabled form control, not listener invocation
for a manually constructed event. **That arm is TV until it runs in a real
browser, and nothing should state it as platform behaviour.** The other three
arms (`.click()` suppressed, enabled control reached, disabled-mid-dispatch
still reaching later listeners) are the ones the break analysis rests on.

### 34.21 The 80b3a046 ruling, documented; and item 5 measured (s40)

Owner ruling on the break s34.19 found: the three guards in `buildHandler`
STAY. Deliberate since v1.6.0 (CHANGELOG v1.6.0 entry and whitepaper s9
beta.15 row, both read at source this window), mirroring Vue #14948 for the
DIRECT listener this directive attaches. On rc.9 a template `@click` registers
first and can veto the dispatch; that is the contract working, and what changed
is who runs first. Documented, not fixed. Landed as `rc9/38`, gate green on the
committed tree, 11 steps.

Four places: both JSDoc sites in `src/directives.ts` (+61 lines, ZERO lines of
code), the v1.22.0 CHANGELOG upgrade note with the per-veto table, one compiled
three-arm case in `tests/directives-vapor-fixture.test.ts` labelled COVERAGE at
the top and argued at the end of the file, and a dated `docs/decisions.md` row.
This closes the last bullet of s34.20 "still owed".

#### The self-review changed three things, all of them from measurement

The tidy reading of the two guard lines is WRONG and is not what shipped.
"267 covers form controls, 268 covers `<a>`/`<div>`/`<span>`" is false:
`.disabled` is a real boolean only on form controls and `undefined` on the
others, but an assignment there is an EXPANDO that reads `true`, so the
property guard fires on every tag. Measured across seven tags through the real
directive (`.probes/disabled-guards.test.ts`). The aria line is simply the only
signal the non-form tags have.

And WHERE line 267 does its work is not where it looks. Reconciled against the
parallel session's `.probes/disabled-click.test.ts`, which measured the channel
mine did not separate: a form control disabled BEFORE the click reaches no
listener at all, so the read never runs for it. It earns its place on the two
channels the platform leaves open - a tag the platform never suppresses, and a
control disabled DURING the dispatch, where the event is already in flight and
is NOT retracted (`["first","second"]`: a listener after the one that disabled
the element still runs). The second is exactly the rc.9 ordering, so 267 is the
line that reads a template handler's veto. Neither JSDoc would have said this
without that probe.

One row was CUT from the CHANGELOG table for being unmeasured: `aria-disabled`
was measured for the directive alone, never for a template `@click` setting it
mid-click, which is what that table is about.

#### Reconciliation with the parallel session

Its four probes were read and re-run here. Every shared figure agrees: the
`typeof` split per tag; directive-first `["cartAdd"]` against template-first
`[]`; `.delegate` unaffected in BOTH orders; direct `stopPropagation()`
surviving where `.delegate` does not. Two independent measurements of the same
table, which is the strongest thing this cycle has produced about any row.
Its `disabled-click` `dispatchEvent` arm stays TV per s34.20 (happy-dom only)
and nothing documented here rests on it.

#### PROCESS: the gate step-9 diagnosis on record was wrong

It was reported failing at step 9 "only because it ran docs before the build".
It is not that. `npm run docs` is idempotent here - two consecutive runs, byte
identical diff - and `scripts/gate.mjs` step 9 is
`git diff --exit-code -- docs/api` AGAINST HEAD, so it fires on any uncommitted
docs/api change whether stale or not. Step 11 wants a clean tree for the same
reason. The gate is built to run on a COMMITTED tree; commit first, gate after.
Running build before docs is still right, it is simply not what was failing.

#### Item 5, measured and PENDING OWNER: the plugin on a Vapor app

`createVaporApp(App).use(createDirectivePlugin())` registers the vDOM OBJECT
directive on a Vapor app. rc.9 warns and skips, so every `v-vc:command` is
inert; rc.8 threw a TypeError at mount. Our JSDoc says to use `vcCommandVapor`
there, in prose, with nothing enforcing it.

DETECTION IS AVAILABLE and needs no `vue` import: measured, a Vapor app carries
an own `vapor` key and a vDOM app does not (own-key diff `["vapor"]` against
`[]`), while `_context` has the identical key set on both. One property read.

Cost, one build each on `dist/directives2.js`, baseline 7,172 raw / 1,946
brotli; the three IIFEs carry no directives code and are unaffected:

    A  auto-register vcCommandVapor        +84 raw / +11 br
    B  throw our own message              +170 raw / +46 br
    C  DEV-only warn, install unchanged   +174 raw / +39 br, folds to 0 in a
                                          consumer's production build

RECOMMENDED C. A silently changes what `.use()` does and, in the shape measured,
drops `v-vc:payload` / `v-vc:optimistic`, which have no Vapor port, so it would
half-work. B turns a silent misconfiguration into a hard install failure against
repo precedent - `warnUnwired` and this same file's below-floor-Vue guard both
warn and continue. C names the fix where Vue's own warning names only the
symptom. Test cost: one case installing on a Vapor app, asserting the message in
DEV and silence under `NODE_ENV=production` - the pattern that file already has,
and the pair covers both arms of the new branch for the 100% gate.

NOT IMPLEMENTED. Owner decides.

#### The four Suspense rows returned by s34.15

These came back because three src comments state a CONDITION against Suspense
rather than mentioning it: `chamber-vapor.ts:273-278` (an awaiting

called after the `await` arm cleanup on the RESTORED scope, including when the
app unmounts during the await), `chamber.ts:16-29` (rc.2 #15141), and
`chamber-vapor.ts:187`.

All four are runtime-core boundary and hydration bookkeeping. None touches scope
restoration, which is what our condition rests on - and that condition is pinned
by a COMMITTED test that runs on rc.9 and passes:
`tests/async-vapor-setup-fixture.test.ts`, whose cases include "leaves no
listener behind when the app unmounts DURING the await". That is the V criterion
met by a test rather than by reading.

       changed: `patchSuspense` brackets a same-type branch patch with
                `suspense.deps++` / `deps--`, so a nested suspensible boundary
                resolving mid-patch cannot resolve the parent before later
                siblings have registered their async deps.

       changed: when an async dep resolves after its component was unmounted, the

### 34.23 The two checks the cycle owed, run at last (s40)

Both were owed and neither had been run. The second is the more serious
omission: every directive test in this repo runs WITHOUT the interop plugin
this package re-exports, which was known and had been left standing.

#### The `<template v-for :key>` check: 01dba84c, 37b85739, 052c7688

s34.18 point 3 pulled these back to plain TV on the right argument - a grep for
`withVaporDirectives` cannot see how key and order codegen reach us. The path
is: keys decide whether an element carrying `v-vc:command` is REUSED or
RECREATED, that decides when the directive scope is disposed, and that decides
whether `unmountCommand` runs at all - with `.delegate`, whether the
per-document refcount is decremented.

So MOUNTS AND CLEANUPS ARE COUNTED, not inferred from whether a click works.
A click still working is exactly what a stranded listener looks like.
`.probes/vfor-keys-and-interop.test.ts`, compiled template, real mounts:

    <ul><template v-for="it in items" :key="it.id">
      <li><button v-vc:command="it.act">{{ it.id }}</button></li>
    </template></ul>

    step                              mounts  cleanups  note
    mount [a,b,c]                        3        0
    keyed reorder -> [c,b,a]             3        0      element REUSED, moved
                                                         to index 2; directive
                                                         did not re-run
    click new index 0                                    -> addC: the binding
                                                         travelled with its node
    filter out the middle                3        1      exactly one
    app.unmount()                        3        3      BALANCED

Identical in BOTH host shapes (plugin absent and installed). The `.delegate`
variant of the same list: 3 mounts / 3 cleanups, and document click listeners
added 1 / removed 1 - no stranded shared listener.

So the three rows are answered for this library: keyed reuse across a reorder
does not re-run the directive and does not leak, and a filtered row runs its
cleanup once. `01dba84c` and `37b85739` move to V; `052c7688` was already V
and its consumer effect is now measured rather than owed.

#### The interop host shape, which had never been exercised

`withVaporDirectives` has two host shapes and the suite only ever drove one.
Installing `vaporInteropPlugin` flips `isInteropEnabled`, which is what gives
the rc.9 root-chain visitor its `onInteropFragment` arm (e35e9af1). Re-run
under it:

    compiled click, plugin installed   -> ["cartAdd"]
    after app.unmount()                -> []          (listener gone)
    mounts / cleanups                  -> 1 / 1
    the whole v-for table above        -> identical to the plugin-free run

No difference found. That is the point of running it: the claim "our directive
behaves the same in a mixed tree" was previously untested in the only host
shape where it could have failed.

#### `v-vc:payload` / `v-vc:optimistic` have no Vapor port - where that is said

The claim was used as an argument in the item-5 proposal, so it is sourced.
It IS documented, in three places, and it is accurate:

    src/directives.ts:461-462   "`v-vc:payload` and `v-vc:optimistic` remain
                                vDOM registrations; in Vapor the payload
                                travels as `data-vc-payload`" (and from there
                                into the generated docs/api)
    docs/whitepaper.md:786      the same sentence
    examples/feature-directives.html:22-23   the same, in the example header

NOT in the ROADMAP feature matrix (line 853 carries one row,
`createDirectivePlugin` - `v-vc:command` directive (vDOM)`) and not in the
README subpath table (line 1286, `v-vc:command` only). Neither states anything
FALSE; neither lists the other two directives at all.

AND THE TWO HALVES ARE NOT EQUAL, which none of the three sentences says.
`v-vc:payload` has a documented substitute - `data-vc-payload`, and a consumer
can bind it. `v-vc:optimistic` has NO substitute: `state.optimisticFn` is set
only by the vDOM `vc-optimistic` registration, so optimistic updates with
rollback are UNAVAILABLE in a Vapor template. That is a capability gap, not a
documentation one, and it is bigger than most of what this cycle has turned up.
Flagged for the owner, not designed here.

### 34.24 The three reactivity/shared rows read, and the TV-N sign-off (s41)

The owner accepted the TV-N batch on the condition that these three be read in
full first, on a stated argument: the other 37 are bumps, dependency updates,
compiler-sfc internals and compiler-only codegen, none of which reaches this
library, while these three sit in `reactivity` and `shared` and therefore end
up inside a consumer's bundle. That is the right cut, and it is the house rule
applied to exactly the rows where something could hide.

    V  599f35bb  fix(reactivity): preserve readonly wrappers in array copy methods (#15469)
       changed: `reactiveReadArray` (arrayInstrumentations.ts) gains two
                readonly-only branches. Before: `isShallow(array) ? raw :
                raw.map(toReactive)`. After: shallow still returns `raw`;
                NON-readonly still returns `raw.map(toReactive)`, the same
                expression; only `isReadonly(array)` takes the new paths,
                `raw.map(item => toReadonly(toReactive(item)))` or
                `raw.map(toReadonly)`.
       breaks : no, and the speed question it raises does not land either.
                THE DEFAULT PATH NEVER REACHES THIS FUNCTION. `applyVueModule`
                wires `signal()` to `shallowRef`, so `useCommandState`'s value
                is the RAW array, not a reactive proxy, and array methods on it
                are the platform's. Only the opt-in `vapor-chamber/reactive`
                subpath (`deepSignal` / `useDeepCommandState`, backed by Vue's
                deep `ref()`) produces a reactive array at all - and there
                `isReadonly` is false, so the executed expression is identical
                to rc.8 plus one `isReadonly()` call per invocation. The new
                branches are unreachable from `src`: `readonly(` is 0 hits, and
                this library freezes with `Object.freeze` / `freezeDeep`
                (src/freeze.ts), never with Vue's readonly proxy.
       enables: nothing. Carried into the ab:vue plan anyway, as the owner asked:
                it is the first row this cycle with even a plausible effect on a
                benched path, so the array row is read rather than assumed.

    V  718f782b  fix(shared): handle circular references in looseEqual (#15462 area)
       changed: `looseEqual` gains an optional `seen` state; array and object
                comparison route through `looseCompareNested`, which memoises
                the pair in two Maps and answers a revisit by identity, so a
                cycle terminates instead of recursing. The object comparison
                body moves out into `looseCompareObjects` unchanged.
       breaks : no. `looseEqual` is 0 hits in `src`. This library's own equality
                is `commandKey`'s canonical serialization and `sameQueryValue`
                in the router - neither calls into shared.
       enables: nothing. Tempting and wrong: `commandKey` is not a looseEqual
                substitute, and a cycle in a payload is a JSON-serialization
                problem there, not a comparison one.

    V  b9456cb4  fix(shared): preserve comment-like text in style values (#15471)
       changed: `parseStringStyle`'s comment regex is widened to match quoted
                strings and escapes FIRST, and the replacer keeps anything that
                is not a `/* */` comment - so `--label: 'a/*b*/c'` survives.
       breaks : no. `normalizeStyle` is 0 hits; this library writes classes and
                one DOM property and never a style binding, and ships no
                `<style>` of its own.
       enables: nothing.

NOT A FLOOR, and calling it one was an overclaim I was pulled up on. Only ONE

establishes a minimum. The +34 raw is itself unexplained - an empty if-block
with no side effects should be dead-code-eliminated outright, which would read

build. The message is most of it.
The shipped form is 43% cheaper in brotli than the sentence it replaced, and
it is the one that matches the division of labour: Vue says what happened,

#### The IIFE budgets have ZERO bytes of headroom. All six.

Measured with the budgets parsed out of `scripts/check-size.mjs` rather than
retyped, so the figure cannot drift from what CI enforces:

    vapor-chamber.iife.min.js           40944 / 40944 (0 B)   12094 / 12094 (0 B)
    vapor-chamber-core.iife.min.js      27891 / 27891 (0 B)    8177 /  8177 (0 B)
    vapor-chamber-elements.iife.min.js  29655 / 29655 (0 B)    8673 /  8673 (0 B)

That is by construction, not by accident: every raise recorded in that file
sets the budget TO the measurement ("the full and elements budgets follow the
measurement down"). One byte reaching any IIFE fails CI. Worth stating in the
log because `npm run size:check` prints KB to one decimal and says "under
budget" - which cannot distinguish 0 bytes of room from 50.

1. I REPORTED AN APPROVAL THAT WAS NEVER GIVEN. The owner approved option C;

### 34.26 The directive reshape: the selector moved into the name (s42, 2026-09-22)

Implemented from `directive-reshape-plan.md`, which is DELETED with this
section - its scope table, its traps and its two owner questions are all
resolved or carried below, so nothing is lost by removing it.

#### What landed

`v-vc:command` is `v-vc-command`; `app.directive('vc', ...)` is
`app.directive('vc-command', ...)`. The other two names were ALREADY hyphenated,
so exactly ONE registration was renamed and everything else in the diff is
deletion or documentation. The plan's framing of "the naming split" was loose
and the owner corrected it: the split was `vc` versus `vc-*`, which is precisely
why `v-vc:payload` looked plausible enough to be written into four-plus sites.

Deleted with the argument read: the `argument()` call, the DEV non-getter guard,
the below-floor Vue version warning that existed only to explain that guard,
both `binding.arg` guards, and the documented `v-vc:[kind]` latch.

OWNER DECISIONS, both taken before any code: no deprecation alias (the alias
would keep the whole machinery whose removal is the point, leave two spellings,
and turn a saving into a cost against budgets at zero headroom; there are no
users), and 4a deferred to a sized analysis AFTER this landed, so the rename's
number stands on its own.

#### Bytes, and where the plan's number was right and incomplete

Same harness as the prototype - a Vite production consumer bundle of
`./directives`, esbuild minify, brotli q11 (`.probes/reshape-bytes.mjs`). The
owner flagged this before measurement: the plan's -147 came from that method and
NOT from `dist/directives2.js`, and mixing the two is the repo's own
"size guards must name their bundler" trap.

    point                                  raw      brotli   vs A
    A  baseline (committed tree)           21,722   7,126    -
    A' code change only                    21,335   6,979    -387 / -147
    B1 + the docblock rewrite              21,335   6,979    0 / 0 vs A'
    B  + the two DEV message strings       21,335   6,982    -387 / -144

The A side reproduced the plan's "as shipped" figure to the BYTE, and A'
reproduced its -147 to the byte. The shipped figure is **-144**, and the 3 bytes
are attributable: two DEV message strings carry the notation, are identical in
length, and compress 3 bytes worse. The prototype touched neither, which is what
the plan warned about when it said to re-measure.

B1 was taken specifically to make the docblock's cost falsifiable rather than
assumed. Comments do not reach the bundle: 0/0.

All three IIFEs are byte-identical at every point. The owner verified
independently that none of them contains any directives code, which makes them a
CONTROL rather than a box to tick - movement would have meant a leak.

#### Two rows moved in docs/BUNDLE-SIZES.md, and only one was expected

`./directives` 17.8 -> 17.7 KB minified is the change. `./vitest` also moved,
90.9 -> 90.8 min and 30.0 -> 29.9 gzip, and `src/vitest.ts` reaches
`src/directives.ts` through no static import - so it needed attributing rather
than rounding away.

What it is, read off the build rather than reasoned: the `./vitest` row bundles
THREE chunks, and the 81,737 B shared one holds `dist/index.js` - the whole
barrel - together with `dist/directives2.js`, `chamber.js`, `transports.js` and
the rest. The barrel statically imports the directives module, so the test entry
carries it. The row moved for a real reason and the reshape genuinely shrinks it.

THE WRONG TURN IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING. Three hand-rolled instruments in a row
said "no contact", and each was wrong in a way that read like a clean answer:

- a source-level import walker over `src/` - reported directives.ts unreachable
  from vitest.ts. It follows relative specifiers only, and the row is built from
  `dist/`, not `src/`;
- a specifier scan over `dist/vitest.js` and `dist/vitest-pure.js` - reported no
  path to `directives2.js`. `vitest-pure.js` reports ZERO specifiers, which
  should itself have been the tell for a 14 KB file;
- a dist-wide hash diff - correctly showed that only four `directives*` files
  changed, which I then read as PROOF the vitest row could not move, and
  published the inference "the row is not a pure function of its inputs". That
  was a claim about the tool, made from my own broken walk, and it was false.

What settled it was reproducing `measure-size.mjs`'s own build with its own
options and printing the chunks with their module lists
(`.probes/vitest-chunks.mjs`). The lesson is the one this file keeps recording
from a different angle: a negative from an instrument nobody has validated is
evidence about the instrument. Reading `scripts/measure-size.mjs` in full is what
should have come first, and the owner said so.

A LEAD, not chased: `./vitest` carries the entire barrel, so importing the test
entry costs the whole library. Whether that is intended is a separate question
from this change, which only made it 0.1 KB smaller.

#### Fails-before, and a contrast worth keeping

40 tests across 4 files: `directives.test.ts` 21,
`directives-vapor-fixture.test.ts` 17, `vapor-hydration-fixture.test.ts` 1,
`vapor-keepalive-fixture.test.ts` 1.

Set that against #15490, a semantically identical break in the same directive,
which failed ZERO tests. The difference is `tests/compile-vapor.ts` and the fact
that a NAME is resolved where an argument was merely compared.

#### The plan's scope table was wrong, and the scan is the scope

The plan listed 9 files; a node scan found the notation in 30 tracked files. The
owner accepted the scan as the scope. The two that mattered most were
`scripts/check-example-directive.mjs` and
`scripts/check-vue-tsc-directive-gap.mjs` - both ENCODE the notation and would
have failed late, after the docs pass. The first matches the example's button by
its LABEL TEXT, so the label and the regex are a coupled pair.

Two further corrections found by reading: `tests/vapor-directives-fixture.test.ts`
has no `withDirs` and never names our directive in code (its hits are prose), and
`tests/vapor-hydration-fixture.test.ts`, `tests/vapor-keepalive-fixture.test.ts`,
`tests/perf.bench.ts` and `tests/compile-vapor.ts` all needed changes the table
did not list.

#### MEASURED CONSEQUENCE the plan did not predict: the vue-tsc gap closes

s34.6 recorded that vue-tsc type-checks the template call by passing the ARGUMENT
as a raw string, so the imported-binding form fails `vue-tsc --noEmit` with
TS2345. With no argument there is nothing to mis-check.

**THE FIRST A/B THAT "PROVED" THIS WAS INVALID, and the reason is the finding.**
It used `import { vcCommandVapor as vVc }` on BOTH arms. Vue resolves an SFC
directive by camelCasing the FULL name: `v-vc:command` looks for `vVc` (the
directive is `vc`, `command` is its argument), but `v-vc-command` looks for
`vVcCommand`. So on the hyphen arm there was no declaration to resolve, and
vue-tsc passed for exactly the "nothing to check" reason the app-wide workaround
passed - the outcome the probe existed to distinguish. A TS2339 control proved
the template was checked for EXPRESSIONS, which is not the same as proving the
directive binding resolved. The conclusion happened to be right; the evidence did
not support it.

Re-run with each arm naming its own binding, and a fourth arm for the failure
mode that misled the first run:

    OLD colon  v-vc:command   binding vVc          exit 2   TS2345 on the argument
    NEW hyphen v-vc-command   binding vVcCommand   exit 0   clean
    CONTROL    hyphen + a deliberate type error    exit 2   TS2339 - and the message
                                                            ENUMERATES vVcCommand's
                                                            full signature, which is
                                                            what proves the binding
                                                            is in scope and resolved
    CONTROL    hyphen + the WRONG binding (vVc)    exit 0   passes silently, which is
                                                            why arm 2 alone proved
                                                            nothing

The third arm is the one the first attempt lacked. Corroborated independently and
more strongly by the example itself: `examples/vapor-sfc` now imports the binding,
uses it, passes its own `vue-tsc --noEmit` build step, and its control genuinely
dispatches under `npm run check:example`.

**The tool has NOT caught up** - the colon form still fails and any directive
taking an argument still trips it. What changed is that this package no longer
has the shape that triggers it.

#### THE BINDING NAME CHANGES, and it fails silently - caught by one gate

`v-vc-command` resolves to `vVcCommand`, not `vVc`. An import left aliased to
`vVc` COMPILES, TYPE-CHECKS AND MOUNTS NOTHING: the same silent dead control
#15490 produced, reintroduced by the fix for it.

It happened here. The example was switched to the imported form with the old
alias, `vue-tsc` passed, `vite build` passed, and
`scripts/check-example-directive.mjs` caught it - it clicks the built page's
button and asserts `vc-loading` and `disabled`. Nothing else in the repo would
have. That script was written after #15490 for precisely this failure mode, and
it has now paid for itself twice.

It is an upgrade note in the CHANGELOG, not a footnote.

#### The vue-tsc gap check: DELETED (owner, 2026-09-22)

`npm run check:tsc-gap` and `scripts/check-vue-tsc-directive-gap.mjs` are gone,
and `examples/vapor-sfc` is back on the imported form.

Settled by history rather than by argument. The option of "keep app-wide
registration, it is also a legitimate style" rests on a false premise: `f902a15`
(rc9/12) is the commit that CREATED the directive usage in that example, and it
chose app-wide registration because of the gap. There is no prior art to
preserve.

Rejected, with reasons worth keeping:

- re-pointing the script at the colon form as an upstream tracker - it would
  track a shape this package no longer uses, so it could only ever go red for a
  reason that cannot affect us. That is an assertion that cannot fail, in mirror
  image, and its red is more convincing than a green-forever guard's silence;
- leaving it red - "that one's always 2" is how a gate stops being read.

On the script's own header, which says to delete it when it fails "because the
tool caught up": the reason did not happen but the instruction still holds. A
reopen condition that fires for an unanticipated cause is still doing its job -
it became inconclusive at precisely the moment it stopped being able to tell us
anything.

WHAT IS NOT DELETED WITH IT: the knowledge. The upstream bug still exists, and
the CHANGELOG now carries one sentence saying the gap is unreachable through
`v-vc-command` because there is no argument to mistype. That is evidence for the
architecture decision - the reshape removed a class of upstream exposure, not
just our own defect - and it is the cost any future argument-based notation has
to answer for.

#### Coverage of a file the gate does not measure

`src/directives.ts` is on the coverage exclusion list (vitest.config.ts documents
why at length), so the gate's 100 x4 is green and SILENT about the one file this
change touches. Measured separately with the exclusion lifted, A/B in the main
tree (a worktree was tried and discarded - with node_modules symlinked the suite
deep-imports `vue` and 111 tests fail, the batch-audit trap from the other side):

    BEFORE  statements 168/173  branches 124/132  functions 22/23  lines 137/140
    AFTER   statements 159/164  branches 113/121  functions 22/23  lines 131/134

The ABSOLUTE uncovered counts are identical - 5 statements, 8 branches, 3 lines,
before and after. The percentages dip 0.15 / 0.55 / 0.09 purely because the
denominator shrank: the change deleted 9 covered statements, 11 covered branches
and 6 covered lines and nothing else. The residue is the documented defensive
guards the config already lists.

#### Section 4b, done: the test that would have caught the drift

Every vDOM directive test resolved BY NAME, so none compiled the notation a
consumer writes - which is how a documented-but-inert `v-vc:payload` survived.
`tests/compile-vapor.ts` gains `compileVdom`, and two cases compile the real
template and assert the payload and the optimistic rollback reach the dispatch.

The drift, shown by the compiler rather than described:

    OLD  v-vc:payload  ->  [_directive_vc, _ctx.p, "payload"]      one shared
                           resolveDirective("vc"), arg rejected by mounted
    NEW  v-vc-payload  ->  resolveDirective("vc-payload"), its own

#### Arming, and a harness fault that produced a false green

Six injections, each breaking what one case guards, restored in a `finally` by
in-memory file copy rather than `git checkout` (s34.25 correction 3).

The first run reported one case "STILL PASSED - NOT ARMED". That was my harness,
not the test: `vitest -t` takes a REGEX, the filter
`v-vc-command + v-vc-payload` contains a `+` quantifier, it matched nothing,
every case was reported `skipped`, and counting "no failures" called that a
pass. A filter that selects nothing looks exactly like a test that cannot be
broken - the same shape as s34.14's false all-clear and s34.25 correction 2. The
harness now escapes the filter AND treats a run that executed zero cases as an
error. All six arm.

#### PROCESS: a truncated log produced a wrong published number

`npx vitest run | tail -60` was used to capture the fails-before, and the tally
then read the truncated log and reported **3 failing tests** when the real figure
was **40**. The owner caught it. Full output goes to a file and the file is read;
nothing is piped into `tail` at the point of capture.

#### Open, for the owner

1. **4a**, deferred by decision, not parked: one directive plus attributes, or
   keep three? The thread to pull is s34.23 - `v-vc-optimistic` has NO Vapor
   substitute, and that asymmetry is the case for 4a. A sized analysis is owed
   before any code.
2. **The vue-tsc gap check is CLOSED, not open** - deleted by owner decision
   on 2026-09-22; see the subsection above. Listed here because this item was
   open when the section was first written.
3. The ritual's step 8 remains owed from s34.7 and is NOT this section's work:
   the whitepaper's rc.9 row, and ROADMAP's dates, peer statements, support

### 34.27 Section 4a implemented: the two vDOM-only directives ported to Vapor (s43, 2026-09-22)

Owner decision: **B2**. Sized first with no code (three options), then built.

#### The sizing that decided it

    option                                   raw     brotli   vs keeping as-is
    C  keep as-is                          21,546    7,049    -
    A  drop the two vDOM-only              21,127    6,973    -419 /  -76
    B1 port, order-dependent               22,217    7,164    +671 / +115
    B2 port, order-independent             22,039    7,168    +493 / +119
    B2 AS SHIPPED                          22,046    7,174    +500 / +125

Vite production consumer bundle of `./directives`, brotli q11, entry
`import * as` so the new exports count - so the absolute numbers differ from
rc9/56's named-import figure and only the deltas compare. The prototype
predicted the shipped cost to within 7 raw / 6 brotli. `./directives` is not
budgeted; all three IIFEs are byte-identical, which is the control. The `.d.ts`
grows 3,238 B / 65 lines for two exports, and reaches no consumer's bundle.

**THE OWNER CORRECTED THE PLAN'S FRAMING, and the correction changed the
answer's shape.** `directive-reshape-plan.md` posed 4a as "one directive plus
attributes, or keep three" - two options, both of which remove or keep. The
asymmetry can be removed in EITHER direction, and levelling UP was never
offered. B is the option the plan did not contain.

#### What the measurement corrected in my own analysis

I expected `data-vc-payload` to be weak on REACTIVITY. It is not: a bound
`:data-vc-payload="JSON.stringify(...)"` tracks a changing payload on BOTH
renderers, because the attribute is read fresh at dispatch. Measured, not
assumed.

Where it is actually weak is FIDELITY, and that is what defeats option A:

    value in the payload    via the binding        via data-vc-payload
    Date                    Date instance          ISO string
    Map                     Map instance           {}
    class instance          instance               plain object
    function                function               DROPPED
    key set to undefined    key present            key GONE

And for optimistic there is no substitute at all. A Vapor consumer had to drop
`v-vc-command` for that button and hand-roll the dispatch, losing
`vc-loading` / `vc-error`, disable-while-busy, the re-entrancy guard, the
timeout and the modifiers - and could not recover them by adding an `@click`
beside the directive, because `80b3a046` makes that handler register FIRST and
`buildHandler`'s guards let it veto the dispatch. So A is not removing
redundancy; it is removing capability and asking consumers to rebuild the rest
by hand. Under the no-removal-for-zero-consumers rule it needed a positive case
and did not have one.

#### B2 over B1, staged rather than argued

A compiled Vapor template applies an element's directives in SOURCE order, and
`mountCommand()` opens by calling `unmountCommand()`, which DELETES the
element's state. So the naive port loses

    <button v-vc-payload="p" v-vc-command="a">

- the button dispatches with no payload, silently. B1 can only warn about it,
and attribute order is not something a consumer has reason to think matters.
B2 holds an unclaimed getter in a weak `pendingVapor` map that `mountCommand`
claims BEFORE building the state literal (so the two slots stay part of the one
hidden-class shape the literal's own note requires).

STEP 1 was to verify that before anything else was written, on compiled
templates in both orders. It passes both, and reducing the shipped code to B1's
shape fails exactly the payload-before-command case - from TWO independent
injections, dropping the slot and dropping the pickup. Four brotli bytes.

#### Arming

Five injections into `src/directives.ts`, each restored by in-memory copy in a
finally with the restore asserted identical. All five went red and named their
case: the B1 reduction, the pickup, the dispatch-time payload read, the
dispatch-time optimistic read, and reading the payload at mount instead of
dispatch.

Two cases in the new block are labelled in the file as NOT fails-before: the
SFC alias test pins upstream compiler behaviour, and the assignability test is
a type assertion that fails at `tsc`.

#### The rc9/57 alias rule, carried

The bindings are `vVcPayload` and `vVcOptimistic`. A shorter alias compiles,
type-checks, and falls back to `resolveDirective("vc-payload")`, which finds
nothing - Vue warns in DEV and the warning is STRIPPED from the production build
the example ships, which is exactly how the same mistake reached a built example
in rc9/57.

Three places now carry it: an upgrade note, the example, and two guards. The
example's optimistic function bumps by `cmd.payload?.qty ?? 1` and the binding
carries `qty: 3`, so one observable answers both directives AND discriminates -
`npm run check:example` reads 3 when the payload is live and 1 when it is dead.
Armed by renaming each alias in the example: the payload arm reports
`optimistic 0 -> 1, expected 3`, the optimistic arm reports `stayed at 0`.

#### A gate gap found on the way, and closed

`rc9/58` added `scripts/check-console-shape.mjs` to `lint:check` but not to
`scripts/gate.mjs`, whose lint step enumerates its pieces BY HAND. So a
console-shape violation would have passed every `npm run gate` run. Added, with
the reason written at the list, because the list invites exactly this.
